One only needs to examine the shift in the company’s capital structure to witness how quickly Apple can adapt to its environment. Capital structure is simply a measure of how much equity and debt a company utilizes to finance its operations. Equity represents ownership in a company and is calculated by finding the sum of the common stock and retained earnings, less the amount of treasury shares. The second component of a company’s capital structure is debt, representing how much the company owes to creditors. At the end of fiscal year 2021, Apple had a debt-to-equity ratio of 1.82. A company might buy back its shares to boost the value of its stock and to improve its financial statements. Companies tend to repurchase shares when they have cash on hand and the stock market is on an upswing. The stock’s price often rises as a result of a buyback but there’s a risk that it will fall. A share repurchase is a reduction in the number of a public company’s shares outstanding. Share repurchases automatically increase the earnings per share (EPS) of the stock by reducing the number of outstanding shares. A share repurchase reduces the number of shares outstanding so it increases earnings per share (EPS). Research and development (R&D) is a company\'s effort to explore technological advancements and scientific research to gain competitive advantages. ## What Is Research and Development (R&D)? Research and development (R&D) helps companies remain competitive by driving innovation and improving products. The concept of research and development is widely linked to innovation in both the corporate and government sectors. R&D allows a company to stay ahead of its competition. A company might not survive on its own and may have to rely on other ways to innovate without an R&D program such as engaging in mergers and acquisitions (M&A) or partnerships. The research and/or development is typically not performed with the expectation of immediate profit. A company might buy back its shares to boost the value of its stock and to improve its financial statements. "follow_up_questions": null, "answer": "Capital expenditures (CapEx) are the funds a company spends to acquire, upgrade, or extend the life of long‑term assets such as land, buildings, equipment, and machinery; these outlays are recorded as assets on the balance sheet rather than as immediate expenses\u202f【https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_expenditure】.  In the United States, the Bureau of Economic Analysis defines CapEx as expenditures made to acquire, add to, or improve property, plant, and equipment (PP&E), and it is measured on a gross basis without netting sales of fixed assets\u202f【https://www.bea.gov/help/glossary/capital-expenditures】.  Recent sector‑level data show that U.S. firms devote the most capital to retail‑general operations (about\u202f$161.6\u202fbillion) and oil and gas production and exploration (approximately\u202f$92.4\u202fbillion), while sectors such as insurance and software spend considerably less\u202f【https://pages.stern.nyu.edu/~adamodar/New_Home_Page/datafile/capex.html】.  The Federal Reserve’s Flow of Funds series tracks total U.S. capital expenditures across all sectors in millions of dollars, providing a macro‑level view of spending trends\u202f【https://fred.stlouisfed.org/series/BOGZ1FA895050005Q】.  Because CapEx represents long‑term investment, it is a key indicator of a firm’s growth strategy and is closely watched by investors for insights into future cash‑flow generation and competitive positioning.", "images": [], "results": [{"url": "https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capital_expenditure", "title": "Capital expenditure - Wikipedia", "content": "*Find sources:*\xa0\\"Capital expenditure\\"\xa0–\xa0news\xa0**·** newspapers\xa0**·** books\xa0**·** scholar\xa0**·** JSTOR *(February 2020)* *(Learn how and when to remove this message)* |  |  | | --- | | The examples and perspectives in this article **deal primarily with the United States and do not represent a worldwide view of the subject**. **Capital expenditure** or **capital expense** (abbreviated **capex**, **CAPEX**, or **CapEx**) is the money an organization or corporate entity spends to buy, maintain, or improve its fixed assets, such as buildings, vehicles, equipment or land. It is considered a capital expenditure when funds are spent to purchase a new asset or to extend the useful life of existing assets, such as major structural repairs or upgrades. In many companies, major capital expenditures require formal approval from the board of directors, depending on corporate governance rules or internal bylaws.” In accounting, a capital expenditure is added to an asset account, thus increasing the asset\'s adjusted basis (the cost or value of an asset adjusted for tax purposes).", "score": 0.5962692, "raw_content": null, "id": "3d2abd-00"}, {"url": "https://www.bea.gov/help/glossary/capital-expenditures", "title": "Capital expenditures | U.S. Bureau of Economic Analysis (BEA)", "content": "An official website of the United States government. ### Main navigation. # Capital expenditures. ## Glossary. Expenditures made to acquire, add to, or improve property, plant, and equipment (PP&E). PP&E includes: land, timber, and minerals; structures, machinery, equipment, special tools, and other depreciable property; construction in progress; and tangible and intangible exploration and development costs. Changes in PP&E due to changes in entity-such as mergers, acquisitions, and divestitures-or to changes in accounting methods are excluded. Capital expenditure (capex) is money you spend to buy, upgrade, or extend the life of [long-term business assets](https://www.xero.com/us/glossary/non-current-assets/) like land, equipment, or buildings. You record capital expenditures as assets on your [balance sheet](https://www.xero.com/us/glossary/balance-sheet/) rather than listing them as expenses. *   You claim annual [depreciation](https://www.xero.com/us/guides/what-is-depreciation/) as a [tax deduction](https://www.xero.com/us/guides/tax-deductions-for-your-business/) over the asset\'s useful life. A capital expenditure budget, similar to your [small business budget](https://www.xero.com/us/guides/create-small-business-budget/), helps you plan major purchases without putting your cash flow at risk. Review your [cash flow forecast](https://www.xero.com/us/templates/cash-flow-forecast-template/) before committing to a capital expenditure, so you know you can cover the cost without straining day-to-day operations. *   [Find an accountant or bookkeeper](https://www.xero.com/us/advisors/). *   [Business resources](https://www.xero.com/us/resources/).", "score": 0.5019781, "raw_content": null, "id": "b98073-03"}, {"url": "https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp", "title": "Understanding Capital Expenditure (CapEx): Definitions ...", "content": "*   [Companies](https://www.investopedia.com/company-news-4427705). *   [](https://www.linkedin.com/company/investopedia-ulc). *   [Companies](https://www.investopedia.com/company-news-4427705). *   [Understanding CapEx](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp#toc-understanding-capital-expenditures-capex). *   [Types](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp#toc-different-types-of-capital-expenditures). *   [Formula and Calculation](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp#toc-calculating-capital-expenditures-formula-and-method). *   [Key Considerations](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp#toc-key-considerations-in-capital-expenditure-analysis). OpEx](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp#toc-capital-expenditures-vs-operating-expenses-key-differences). *   [Examples](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp#toc-real-world-examples-of-capital-expenditures). *   [How to Use CapEx](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp#toc-example-of-how-to-use-capex). *   [FAQs](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp#toc-what-type-of-investment-is-capex). *   [The Bottom Line](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/c/capitalexpenditure.asp#toc-the-bottom-line). It may be listed as capital spending, [PP&E purchases](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/p/ppe.asp), or acquisition expenses. Find the amount of [depreciation](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/d/depreciation.asp) expense recorded for the current period on the income statement. Capital expenditures are also used in calculating [free cash flow to equity](https://www.investopedia.com/terms/f/freecashflowtoequity.asp) (FCFE). Interpretation\n\n*   **Strategic Prioritization of AI Infrastructure**: Microsoft’s capital deployment patterns from FY24 to August 2026 outline a deliberate shift toward supporting generative AI and cloud capacity. The company expanded its annual capital expenditures from $56 billion in FY24 [src: https://www.forbes.com/sites/petercohan/2024/07/31/microsoft-stock-drops-as-ai-capital-expenditures-surge-to-56-billion] to $64.55 billion in FY25 [src: https://finance.yahoo.com/markets/stocks/articles/apple-vs-microsoft-enterprise-capex-163934722.html], and guided for sequential acceleration above $40 billion in Q4 FY26 alone [src: https://finance.yahoo.com/quote/MSF0.F/earnings/MSF0.F-Q3-2026-earnings_call-547930.html]. This massive level of infrastructure investment is justified by the concurrent growth in its high-margin segments: Microsoft Cloud revenue reached $54.5 billion (up 29% YoY) and its AI business run-rate grew 123% YoY to exceed $37 billion in ARR in Q3 FY26 [src: https://finance.yahoo.com/quote/MSF0.F/earnings/MSF0.F-Q3-2026-earnings_call-547930.html].\n*   **Balance Sheet Self-Funding Capability**: Despite the extreme cash intensity of building next-generation data centers, Microsoft\'s capital allocation remains highly balanced. The company generates robust cash flows (such as $46.7 billion in operating cash flow in Q3 FY26 [src: https://finance.yahoo.com/quote/MSFT.SN/earnings/MSFT.SN-Q3-2026-earnings_call-547930.html]), enabling it to entirely self-fund this aggressive CapEx cycle. Concurrently, Microsoft maintained consistent capital returns to shareholders, distributing over $10 billion per quarter via buybacks and dividends throughout FY26 [src: https://finance.yahoo.com/quote/MSFT.SN/earnings/MSFT.SN-Q3-2026-earnings_call-547930.html] [src: https://finance.yahoo.com/markets/stocks/articles/microsoft-q4-earnings-beat-estimates-160100382.html] while simultaneously reducing its long-term debt from $40.3 billion in Q3 FY26 [src: https://finance.yahoo.com/markets/stocks/articles/microsoft-q3-earnings-beat-estimates-155800843.html] to $31.1
